People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'm here because... well, people keep acting like I'm just imagining it. Which is exactly what my sister used to say when we were little. She always told everyone I had this wild imagination, like this one time I swore the neighbor's dog was stealing our garden trowels. We had this beautiful tomato garden back then, big beefsteak tomatoes, the kind that are just perfect for sandwiches... 

But anyway, I am absolutely not imagining this. I know for a fact that the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house. They even follow me on my cleaning routes. I do housekeeping, you know, and you wouldn't believe the dust bunnies people let build up under their guest beds. It's so much bending and reaching, my knees just ache by the end of the week. But yes, they're watching me while I work and watching the house because they want to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. They think they're being quiet about it, but I know what I see.

A head injury? Oh, no, nothing like that. My head is perfectly fine. I mean, I did bump it pretty good on the underside of Mr. Abernathy's dining table a few weeks ago. husband.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Even when the house is quiet, I hear a woman's voice near the kitchen window telling me to check the locks or move his pills, clear as if she is standing right there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: When I try to explain what is happening, the words run from my husband's heart pills to the church schedule to the numbers on my client list, and people say they can't follow me.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: At work or at home I catch myself lining up bottles, towels, and chairs in certain patterns and walking back through the rooms over and over because it feels wrong to leave them any other way.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: There are stretches when I feel bright and strong like I can handle everything, but if my husband or a client questions me I snap much harder than I mean to.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I get moving before daylight, cleaning, cooking, calling about appointments, making new lists, and taking on church errands even when everyone tells me to slow down.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I started feeling like I was the only one with the sense and strength to keep my husband, my home, and even some of the church people safe from what was coming.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: Many nights I sleep two or three hours and wake up ready to scrub floors or sort papers, not tired like I used to be after a short night.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My mind jumps so fast from bills to Bible verses to my husband's breathing to which house I need to clean next that I can't catch one thought long enough to finish it.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a car door shuts outside, the refrigerator hums, or someone moves a cup while I'm talking, my attention goes straight to that and I lose what I was doing.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the weeks when I was sleeping very little and running from cleaning jobs to church errands, I was also hearing that woman's voice and feeling certain people were monitoring my routes.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: For most of this whole change, I have been keyed up, irritable, and driven to keep moving, even in the stretches when things were not at their worst.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a quieter spell for several weeks when I was sleeping more and not rushing around as much, but I still heard the voice by the kitchen window and believed the agency was watching us.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My cleaning agency took away my supervisory routes because clients and coworkers said they could not rely on my work the way they used to. At home, my husband and pastor have had to step in more because I am not keeping up with caregiving and household responsibilities safely.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole change has been going on for about 1 year and 2 months, since after my husband's cardiac hospitalization.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that people are watching us have been happening for about 11 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: The stretches of being unusually energized, irritable, and barely sleeping have been happening on and off for about 10 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / maid_or_housekeeping_cleaner / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. Sometimes when the house is completely quiet... especially in the afternoons. I usually try to sit down with a cup of tea around three o'clock, Earl Grey if we have it, though my husband prefers that chamomile stuff. I don't know how he drinks it, it tastes like old lawn clippings to me. We used to have this lovely tea set with little painted violets on it that my mother gave us for our wedding, but one of the saucers chipped during our last move... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the voices. Well, it's just one voice, really. A woman's voice. She's always over by the kitchen window, right where I keep my African violets. They need bright, indirect light, you know, or the leaves get all spotty. She tells me to check the locks on the doors, or she tells me I need to move his pills. And it's not like a thought in my head, it's clear as day, like she's standing right there next to the sink while I'm trying to water the plants.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? Oh, no, nothing like that. Thank goodness, because I don't know how I'd manage my cleaning schedule if I was having those. I did have a pretty nasty fall last Tuesday, though. I was mopping over at the Henderson place—they have these beautiful original hardwood floors, built in 1924 I think. Or maybe '26. Anyway, Mrs. Henderson insists on me using this special oil soap, and if you don't buff it out just right, it's like walking on an ice rink. \n\nI went right down on my hip and it hurt like the dickens. She gave me a bag of frozen peas to put on it, but honestly, frozen corn works so much better. The kernels are smaller, you see, so they mold to your joints better when you wrap it in a tea towel. I always keep a bag in my own freezer just in case. But no, no seizures or anything like that. Just the bruised hip.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, no, nothing like that. My head is perfectly fine. I mean, I did bump it pretty good on the underside of Mr. Abernathy's dining table a few weeks ago. He has this massive antique oak table, claw feet, it must weigh an absolute ton. I was down on my hands and knees getting the dust off the crossbeams—people never think to dust the crossbeams, but you can see the dust just sitting there if the afternoon light hits it right. \n\nHe has these big bay windows in that room with heavy velvet drapes. I keep telling him he needs to take those drapes down and get them properly dry-cleaned, because velvet holds onto dust and dander like you wouldn't believe. It's just terrible for allergies. My husband's allergies have been acting up so much lately with all the pollen, he's just sneezing constantly when he goes out to get the mail... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my head. No, I've never had a real injury or anything serious. Well, it's been about 11 months now for the voice and that awful feeling that we're being watched. I remember clearly because it started right around the time I was switching out the winter linens over at the Gable house. Mrs. Gable insists on having these incredibly heavy flannel sheets on the beds from November all the way through April, even though her husband just sweats right through them by March. \n\nI have to wash them twice sometimes just to get the smell out. Losing interest? No, I wouldn't say so. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed. Like my baking, for instance. I was just making a lemon pound cake this weekend, and... well, speaking of baking, you wouldn't believe the state of Mrs. Higgins' oven. I was cleaning over there on Thursday, and she asked me to just run the self-clean cycle. But you can't just run the self-clean if there's a thick layer of burnt pie crust and grease sitting at the bottom! It'll smoke out the whole house and set off the fire alarms. 

I had to get right in there with a wet pumice stone. Pumice stones are absolutely wonderful for porcelain oven interiors, you know. You just wet them a little bit and they scrape right through the baked-on carbon without scratching the enamel one bit. People always want to use those harsh chemical sprays, the ones in the bright yellow cans? They smell like pure ammonia and the fumes just linger in the kitchen for days. If you have a parakeet or a canary, those chemical fumes will kill the poor thing right on the spot. My aunt had a canary when I was a little girl, a beautiful little yellow thing named Sunshine, and she kept his cage right by the kitchen window... 

Anyway, what was the question? Oh, right, losing interest. No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I still like doing all my usual things.

Well, yes, I suppose people have mentioned that. When I try to explain what's happening to me, the words just sort of run together. I'll start talking about my husband's heart pills, and then somehow I'm talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list... 

Speaking of my client list, I had to completely reorganize my schedule this week because the Henderson family decided to take a last-minute trip to Florida. They always go to Sanibel Island, which is beautiful, I'm sure, but they leave their poor golden retriever, Buster, at that boarding kennel out on Route 9. I always tell Mrs. Henderson she should just hire a pet sitter to stay at the house. Those kennels are just breeding grounds for kennel cough, even if they're vaccinated. You know, you can get this special enzyme spray for dog beds that completely neutralizes the wet dog smell. You just mist it on and let it air dry, but you have to make sure you get the kind with the live enzymes, not just the perfumes that cover it up. The perfumed ones just make the house smell like a wet dog rolled in a lavender bush, which is honestly worse than just the dog smell to begin with. I bought a bottle for my sister's house because her schnauzer...

Oh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, my speech. Yes, people do say they can't follow me when I try to explain things. They say I jump around too much.

Flatter? No, I don't think my emotions are flat at all. I still feel things just as strongly as I always have. Like when I get frustrated, I really feel it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... well, people keep acting like I'm just imagining it. Which is exactly what my sister used to say when we were little. She always told everyone I had this wild imagination, like this one time I swore the neighbor's dog was stealing our garden trowels. We had this beautiful tomato garden back then, big beefsteak tomatoes, the kind that are just perfect for sandwiches... \n\nBut anyway, I am absolutely not imagining this. I know for a fact that the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house. They even follow me on my cleaning routes. I do housekeeping, you know, and you wouldn't believe the dust bunnies people let build up under their guest beds. It's so much bending and reaching, my knees just ache by the end of the week. But yes, they're watching me while I work and watching the house because they want to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. They think they're being quiet about it, but I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. Sometimes when the house is completely quiet... especially in the afternoons. I usually try to sit down with a cup of tea around three o'clock, Earl Grey if we have it, though my husband prefers that chamomile stuff. I don't know how he drinks it, it tastes like old lawn clippings to me. We used to have this lovely tea set with little painted violets on it that my mother gave us for our wedding, but one of the saucers chipped during our last move... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the voices. Well, it's just one voice, really. A woman's voice. She's always over by the kitchen window, right where I keep my African violets. They need bright, indirect light, you know, or the leaves get all spotty. She tells me to check the locks on the doors, or she tells me I need to move his pills. And it's not like a thought in my head, it's clear as day, like she's standing right there next to the sink while I'm trying to water the plants.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? Oh, no, nothing like that. Thank goodness, because I don't know how I'd manage my cleaning schedule if I was having those. I did have a pretty nasty fall last Tuesday, though. I was mopping over at the Henderson place—they have these beautiful original hardwood floors, built in 1924 I think. Or maybe '26. Anyway, Mrs. Henderson insists on me using this special oil soap, and if you don't buff it out just right, it's like walking on an ice rink. \n\nI went right down on my hip and it hurt like the dickens. She gave me a bag of frozen peas to put on it, but honestly, frozen corn works so much better. The kernels are smaller, you see, so they mold to your joints better when you wrap it in a tea towel. I always keep a bag in my own freezer just in case. But no, no seizures or anything like that. Just the bruised hip.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, no, nothing like that. My head is perfectly fine. I mean, I did bump it pretty good on the underside of Mr. Abernathy's dining table a few weeks ago. He has this massive antique oak table, claw feet, it must weigh an absolute ton. I was down on my hands and knees getting the dust off the crossbeams—people never think to dust the crossbeams, but you can see the dust just sitting there if the afternoon light hits it right. \n\nHe has these big bay windows in that room with heavy velvet drapes. I keep telling him he needs to take those drapes down and get them properly dry-cleaned, because velvet holds onto dust and dander like you wouldn't believe. It's just terrible for allergies. My husband's allergies have been acting up so much lately with all the pollen, he's just sneezing constantly when he goes out to get the mail... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my head. No, I've never had a real injury or anything serious. Just that little bump on the oak table.\n\nDoctor: How long have the voice and the feeling that people are watching you been going on?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about 11 months now for the voice and that awful feeling that we're being watched. I remember clearly because it started right around the time I was switching out the winter linens over at the Gable house. Mrs. Gable insists on having these incredibly heavy flannel sheets on the beds from November all the way through April, even though her husband just sweats right through them by March. \n\nI have to wash them twice sometimes just to get the smell out. I always use a half cup of white vinegar in the rinse cycle—it's an absolute miracle worker for odors, really. You don't even need those expensive fabric softeners that just coat all your clothes in wax. People don't realize it, but if you look at your dryer's lint trap after using those liquid softeners, it's just a sticky, gummy mess. I spent nearly an hour scraping the lint screen at the Gable house just last week with an old toothbrush. You have to use a soft bristle brush, though, or you'll tear the mesh right out of the plastic frame, and replacement parts for those new LG dryers take weeks to ship... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the timeline. Yes, it's been about 11 months since all of that started.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? No, I wouldn't say so. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed. Like my baking, for instance. I was just making a lemon pound cake this weekend, and... well, speaking of baking, you wouldn't believe the state of Mrs. Higgins' oven. I was cleaning over there on Thursday, and she asked me to just run the self-clean cycle. But you can't just run the self-clean if there's a thick layer of burnt pie crust and grease sitting at the bottom! It'll smoke out the whole house and set off the fire alarms. \n\nI had to get right in there with a wet pumice stone. Pumice stones are absolutely wonderful for porcelain oven interiors, you know. You just wet them a little bit and they scrape right through the baked-on carbon without scratching the enamel one bit. People always want to use those harsh chemical sprays, the ones in the bright yellow cans? They smell like pure ammonia and the fumes just linger in the kitchen for days. If you have a parakeet or a canary, those chemical fumes will kill the poor thing right on the spot. My aunt had a canary when I was a little girl, a beautiful little yellow thing named Sunshine, and she kept his cage right by the kitchen window... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right, losing interest. No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I still like doing all my usual things.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose people have mentioned that. When I try to explain what's happening to me, the words just sort of run together. I'll start talking about my husband's heart pills, and then somehow I'm talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list... \n\nSpeaking of my client list, I had to completely reorganize my schedule this week because the Henderson family decided to take a last-minute trip to Florida. They always go to Sanibel Island, which is beautiful, I'm sure, but they leave their poor golden retriever, Buster, at that boarding kennel out on Route 9. I always tell Mrs. Henderson she should just hire a pet sitter to stay at the house. Those kennels are just breeding grounds for kennel cough, even if they're vaccinated. You know, you can get this special enzyme spray for dog beds that completely neutralizes the wet dog smell. You just mist it on and let it air dry, but you have to make sure you get the kind with the live enzymes, not just the perfumes that cover it up. The perfumed ones just make the house smell like a wet dog rolled in a lavender bush, which is honestly worse than just the dog smell to begin with. I bought a bottle for my sister's house because her schnauzer...\n\nOh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, my speech. Yes, people do say they can't follow me when I try to explain things. They say I jump around too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that your emotions seem flatter than they used to?\n\nPatient: Flatter? No, I don't think my emotions are flat at all. I still feel things just as strongly as I always have. Like when I get frustrated, I really feel it. Just yesterday, I was so irritated because I went to restock my cleaning caddy, and someone—I'm looking at my husband—had taken my good microfiber cloths out to the garage to wipe down his truck. \n\nYou cannot use the good indoor microfiber on automotive grease! It completely ruins the fibers. Once you get that black grease in them, they're done for. You can wash them in hot water with degreaser all you want, but they'll just streak the mirrors next time you try to clean a bathroom. I buy those specific blue cloths in bulk from a commercial supply place downtown because they have a tighter weave than the ones you get at the grocery store. The grocery store ones leave little lint fuzzies all over the glass, especially if you use them with a vinegar solution instead of regular glass cleaner. I always tell my younger girls at the agency, if you're going to clean a mirror, you have to fold the cloth into quarters so you have eight clean sides to work with. If you just bunch it up in your hand, you're just smearing the toothpaste splatters around...\n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, my emotions. No, they aren't flat. I still feel everything just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I still feel everything just fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides that woman's voice, have you heard any other voices when no one else was there?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Other voices? No, it's just her. Even when the house is completely quiet, I hear her right near the kitchen window. She tells me to check the locks or move my husband's pills, and it's as clear as if she were standing right next to me. 

Speaking of the kitchen window, I really need to get up there and clean the tracks before winter sets in. The dust just collects in those little grooves, and once the condensation starts building up from the cold weather, it turns into this awful, sticky mud. You really need an old toothbrush to get into the corners properly. My grandmother used to save all her old toothbrushes for cleaning grout and window tracks. She had this whole jar of them under the sink, right next to the Borax. You know, Borax is a miracle worker for getting stains out of porcelain sinks. You just make a little paste with some water and let it sit for ten minutes. It works so much better than bleach, and it doesn't ruin the finish. Bleach will actually yellow white porcelain over time if you use it too much. I see it all the time in these older homes where the owners think they have to bleach everything to death just to feel clean. It's terrible for the plumbing, too, especially if you have a septic system...

Wait, what was the question? Oh, the voices. Yes, just that one woman by the window telling me about the locks and the pills. No one else.

Well, just what I mentioned about the locks and the pills. She doesn't order me to do anything else, if that's what you mean. I'm always checking locks anyway, especially when I'm finishing up at a client's house. 

The Millers just put in one of those fancy smart locks on their front door where you punch in a code instead of using a key. But honestly, they are an absolute nightmare to keep clean. The oils from people's fingers leave these shiny smudges all over the matte black finish. I tried telling Mrs. Miller that a burglar could just look at the greasy spots and guess her code! You can't just wipe it with a wet rag, either, because it streaks terribly. You really need a tiny bit of rubbing alcohol on a utility cloth. And you have to be so careful not to let any liquid seep behind the buttons because it'll short out the whole electrical mechanism. My brother-in-law ruined his garage door keypad exactly like that. He just sprayed the hose right at it while he was power-washing the driveway, and he had to pay two hundred dollars to get a guy out there to replace the whole circuit board... 

I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Did you ask about the locks? Oh, the voice. Right. No, she doesn't tell me to do anything else.

Well, it's just one voice, really. A woman's voice. She's always over by the kitchen window, right where I keep my African violets. They need bright, indirect light, you know, or the leaves get all spotty. She tells me to check the locks on the doors, or she tells me I need to move his pills. And it's not like a thought in my head, it's clear as day, like she's standing right there next to the sink while I'm trying to water the plants.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? Oh, no, nothing like that. Thank goodness, because I don't know how I'd manage my cleaning schedule if I was having those. I did have a pretty nasty fall last Tuesday, though. I was mopping over at the Henderson place—they have these beautiful original hardwood floors, built in 1924 I think. Or maybe '26. Anyway, Mrs. Henderson insists on me using this special oil soap, and if you don't buff it out just right, it's like walking on an ice rink. \n\nI went right down on my hip and it hurt like the dickens. She gave me a bag of frozen peas to put on it, but honestly, frozen corn works so much better. The kernels are smaller, you see, so they mold to your joints better when you wrap it in a tea towel. I always keep a bag in my own freezer just in case. But no, no seizures or anything like that. Just the bruised hip.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, no, nothing like that. My head is perfectly fine. I mean, I did bump it pretty good on the underside of Mr. Abernathy's dining table a few weeks ago. He has this massive antique oak table, claw feet, it must weigh an absolute ton. I was down on my hands and knees getting the dust off the crossbeams—people never think to dust the crossbeams, but you can see the dust just sitting there if the afternoon light hits it right. \n\nHe has these big bay windows in that room with heavy velvet drapes. I keep telling him he needs to take those drapes down and get them properly dry-cleaned, because velvet holds onto dust and dander like you wouldn't believe. It's just terrible for allergies. My husband's allergies have been acting up so much lately with all the pollen, he's just sneezing constantly when he goes out to get the mail... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my head. No, I've never had a real injury or anything serious. Just that little bump on the oak table.\n\nDoctor: How long have the voice and the feeling that people are watching you been going on?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about 11 months now for the voice and that awful feeling that we're being watched. I remember clearly because it started right around the time I was switching out the winter linens over at the Gable house. Mrs. Gable insists on having these incredibly heavy flannel sheets on the beds from November all the way through April, even though her husband just sweats right through them by March. \n\nI have to wash them twice sometimes just to get the smell out. I always use a half cup of white vinegar in the rinse cycle—it's an absolute miracle worker for odors, really. You don't even need those expensive fabric softeners that just coat all your clothes in wax. People don't realize it, but if you look at your dryer's lint trap after using those liquid softeners, it's just a sticky, gummy mess. I spent nearly an hour scraping the lint screen at the Gable house just last week with an old toothbrush. You have to use a soft bristle brush, though, or you'll tear the mesh right out of the plastic frame, and replacement parts for those new LG dryers take weeks to ship... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the timeline. Yes, it's been about 11 months since all of that started.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? No, I wouldn't say so. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed. Like my baking, for instance. I was just making a lemon pound cake this weekend, and... well, speaking of baking, you wouldn't believe the state of Mrs. Higgins' oven. I was cleaning over there on Thursday, and she asked me to just run the self-clean cycle. But you can't just run the self-clean if there's a thick layer of burnt pie crust and grease sitting at the bottom! It'll smoke out the whole house and set off the fire alarms. \n\nI had to get right in there with a wet pumice stone. Pumice stones are absolutely wonderful for porcelain oven interiors, you know. You just wet them a little bit and they scrape right through the baked-on carbon without scratching the enamel one bit. People always want to use those harsh chemical sprays, the ones in the bright yellow cans? They smell like pure ammonia and the fumes just linger in the kitchen for days. If you have a parakeet or a canary, those chemical fumes will kill the poor thing right on the spot. My aunt had a canary when I was a little girl, a beautiful little yellow thing named Sunshine, and she kept his cage right by the kitchen window... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right, losing interest. No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I still like doing all my usual things.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose people have mentioned that. When I try to explain what's happening to me, the words just sort of run together. I'll start talking about my husband's heart pills, and then somehow I'm talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list... \n\nSpeaking of my client list, I had to completely reorganize my schedule this week because the Henderson family decided to take a last-minute trip to Florida. They always go to Sanibel Island, which is beautiful, I'm sure, but they leave their poor golden retriever, Buster, at that boarding kennel out on Route 9. I always tell Mrs. Henderson she should just hire a pet sitter to stay at the house. Those kennels are just breeding grounds for kennel cough, even if they're vaccinated. You know, you can get this special enzyme spray for dog beds that completely neutralizes the wet dog smell. You just mist it on and let it air dry, but you have to make sure you get the kind with the live enzymes, not just the perfumes that cover it up. The perfumed ones just make the house smell like a wet dog rolled in a lavender bush, which is honestly worse than just the dog smell to begin with. I bought a bottle for my sister's house because her schnauzer...\n\nOh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, my speech. Yes, people do say they can't follow me when I try to explain things. They say I jump around too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that your emotions seem flatter than they used to?\n\nPatient: Flatter? No, I don't think my emotions are flat at all. I still feel things just as strongly as I always have. Like when I get frustrated, I really feel it. Just yesterday, I was so irritated because I went to restock my cleaning caddy, and someone—I'm looking at my husband—had taken my good microfiber cloths out to the garage to wipe down his truck. \n\nYou cannot use the good indoor microfiber on automotive grease! It completely ruins the fibers. Once you get that black grease in them, they're done for. You can wash them in hot water with degreaser all you want, but they'll just streak the mirrors next time you try to clean a bathroom. I buy those specific blue cloths in bulk from a commercial supply place downtown because they have a tighter weave than the ones you get at the grocery store. The grocery store ones leave little lint fuzzies all over the glass, especially if you use them with a vinegar solution instead of regular glass cleaner. The dust just collects in those little grooves, and once the condensation starts building up from the cold weather, it turns into this awful, sticky mud. You really need an old toothbrush to get into the corners properly. My grandmother used to save all her old toothbrushes for cleaning grout and window tracks. She had this whole jar of them under the sink, right next to the Borax. You know, Borax is a miracle worker for getting stains out of porcelain sinks. You just make a little paste with some water and let it sit for ten minutes. It works so much better than bleach, and it doesn't ruin the finish. Bleach will actually yellow white porcelain over time if you use it too much. It's terrible for the plumbing, too, especially if you have a septic system...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the voices. Yes, just that one woman by the window telling me about the locks and the pills. No one else.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, just what I mentioned about the locks and the pills. I tried telling Mrs. Miller that a burglar could just look at the greasy spots and guess her code! You can't just wipe it with a wet rag, either, because it streaks terribly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Controlling my thoughts? No, definitely not. My thoughts are completely my own. Though I will say my mind wanders a lot when I'm working on autopilot. 

Like when I'm doing baseboards. You spend a lot of time on your hands and knees doing baseboards, and you really have to use a damp rag, not one of those fluffy dusters. A duster just pushes the dog hair right back into the air, and then it just settles onto the coffee table you just finished polishing. I always tell the new girls at work, you need a small bucket of warm water with just one single drop of Dawn dish soap. Dawn cuts right through that sticky, greasy dust that builds up, especially on the baseboards near the kitchen. But you have to wring the rag out really, really well, or you'll warp the MDF if they have cheap baseboards. So many of these new houses use that cheap pressed wood instead of real solid pine, and the minute water gets under the paint, it swells up like a sponge. I saw it happen at a beautiful new build over in the Heights—the whole hallway trim was completely ruined because the homeowners kept mopping with way too much water on their sponge mop...

I'm sorry, my mind is just running today. Did you ask about my thoughts? Right, no, nobody is controlling them. Just me in there.

If you have a parakeet or a canary, those chemical fumes will kill the poor thing right on the spot. Well, yes, actually. The agency I work for, they took away my supervisory routes recently. They said the clients and the other girls felt they couldn't rely on my work the way they used to. Which is just ridiculous, because I have always been the most thorough one on the team. 

But it's funny they brought up reliability, because reliability is everything when you're cleaning someone's home. You have to show up on time, and you have to remember exactly how they like things done. Mrs. Gable, for instance, she insists her hardwood floors are only cleaned with this special almond oil polish she orders from Germany. You can't use Murphy's Oil Soap, you can't use vinegar and water, it has to be this almond oil. And it smells wonderful, like a bakery, but it makes the floors incredibly slick if you don't buff it out perfectly. I always tell the girls, you have to use a microfiber cloth on the bottom of a flat mop, and you have to go with the grain of the wood. If you go against the grain, you leave these awful streaks that catch the light from the windows. And she has those huge floor-to-ceiling windows in her sunroom, so you see every single flaw. I swear, cleaning windows is an art form in itself. You can't wash windows on a sunny day, you know. The sun dries the cleaner too fast and leaves streaks. You always have to wait for an overcast day...

Oh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Trouble keeping up? Right. Well, it's not just work. At home, my husband and our pastor have had to step in a lot more lately. They say I'm not keeping up with my household chores and caregiving safely. I don't know about "safely," but I suppose I have been falling behind a bit.

We had this beautiful tomato garden back then, big beefsteak tomatoes, the kind that are just perfect for sandwiches... \n\nBut anyway, I am absolutely not imagining this. I know for a fact that the hospital people and some folks from the agency have been watching our house. They even follow me on my cleaning routes. I do housekeeping, you know, and you wouldn't believe the dust bunnies people let build up under their guest beds. It's so much bending and reaching, my knees just ache by the end of the week. But yes, they're watching me while I work and watching the house because they want to see if I'm taking proper care of my husband. They think they're being quiet about it, but I know what I see.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. Sometimes when the house is completely quiet... especially in the afternoons. I usually try to sit down with a cup of tea around three o'clock, Earl Grey if we have it, though my husband prefers that chamomile stuff. I don't know how he drinks it, it tastes like old lawn clippings to me. We used to have this lovely tea set with little painted violets on it that my mother gave us for our wedding, but one of the saucers chipped during our last move... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the voices. Well, it's just one voice, really. A woman's voice. She's always over by the kitchen window, right where I keep my African violets. They need bright, indirect light, you know, or the leaves get all spotty. She tells me to check the locks on the doors, or she tells me I need to move his pills. And it's not like a thought in my head, it's clear as day, like she's standing right there next to the sink while I'm trying to water the plants.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent seizures?\n\nPatient: Seizures? Oh, no, nothing like that. Thank goodness, because I don't know how I'd manage my cleaning schedule if I was having those. I did have a pretty nasty fall last Tuesday, though. I was mopping over at the Henderson place—they have these beautiful original hardwood floors, built in 1924 I think. Or maybe '26. Anyway, Mrs. Henderson insists on me using this special oil soap, and if you don't buff it out just right, it's like walking on an ice rink. \n\nI went right down on my hip and it hurt like the dickens. She gave me a bag of frozen peas to put on it, but honestly, frozen corn works so much better. The kernels are smaller, you see, so they mold to your joints better when you wrap it in a tea towel. I always keep a bag in my own freezer just in case. But no, no seizures or anything like that. Just the bruised hip.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: A head injury? Oh, no, nothing like that. My head is perfectly fine. I mean, I did bump it pretty good on the underside of Mr. Abernathy's dining table a few weeks ago. He has this massive antique oak table, claw feet, it must weigh an absolute ton. I was down on my hands and knees getting the dust off the crossbeams—people never think to dust the crossbeams, but you can see the dust just sitting there if the afternoon light hits it right. \n\nHe has these big bay windows in that room with heavy velvet drapes. I keep telling him he needs to take those drapes down and get them properly dry-cleaned, because velvet holds onto dust and dander like you wouldn't believe. It's just terrible for allergies. My husband's allergies have been acting up so much lately with all the pollen, he's just sneezing constantly when he goes out to get the mail... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my head. No, I've never had a real injury or anything serious. Just that little bump on the oak table.\n\nDoctor: How long have the voice and the feeling that people are watching you been going on?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about 11 months now for the voice and that awful feeling that we're being watched. I remember clearly because it started right around the time I was switching out the winter linens over at the Gable house. Mrs. Gable insists on having these incredibly heavy flannel sheets on the beds from November all the way through April, even though her husband just sweats right through them by March. \n\nI have to wash them twice sometimes just to get the smell out. I always use a half cup of white vinegar in the rinse cycle—it's an absolute miracle worker for odors, really. You don't even need those expensive fabric softeners that just coat all your clothes in wax. People don't realize it, but if you look at your dryer's lint trap after using those liquid softeners, it's just a sticky, gummy mess. I spent nearly an hour scraping the lint screen at the Gable house just last week with an old toothbrush. You have to use a soft bristle brush, though, or you'll tear the mesh right out of the plastic frame, and replacement parts for those new LG dryers take weeks to ship... \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right, the timeline. Yes, it's been about 11 months since all of that started.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Losing interest? No, I wouldn't say so. I still enjoy the things I've always enjoyed. Like my baking, for instance. I was just making a lemon pound cake this weekend, and... well, speaking of baking, you wouldn't believe the state of Mrs. Higgins' oven. I was cleaning over there on Thursday, and she asked me to just run the self-clean cycle. But you can't just run the self-clean if there's a thick layer of burnt pie crust and grease sitting at the bottom! It'll smoke out the whole house and set off the fire alarms. \n\nI had to get right in there with a wet pumice stone. Pumice stones are absolutely wonderful for porcelain oven interiors, you know. You just wet them a little bit and they scrape right through the baked-on carbon without scratching the enamel one bit. People always want to use those harsh chemical sprays, the ones in the bright yellow cans? They smell like pure ammonia and the fumes just linger in the kitchen for days. If you have a parakeet or a canary, those chemical fumes will kill the poor thing right on the spot. My aunt had a canary when I was a little girl, a beautiful little yellow thing named Sunshine, and she kept his cage right by the kitchen window... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right, losing interest. No, no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I still like doing all my usual things.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is hard to follow or jumps from topic to topic?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose people have mentioned that. When I try to explain what's happening to me, the words just sort of run together. I'll start talking about my husband's heart pills, and then somehow I'm talking about the church schedule, and then the numbers on my client list... \n\nSpeaking of my client list, I had to completely reorganize my schedule this week because the Henderson family decided to take a last-minute trip to Florida. They always go to Sanibel Island, which is beautiful, I'm sure, but they leave their poor golden retriever, Buster, at that boarding kennel out on Route 9. I always tell Mrs. Henderson she should just hire a pet sitter to stay at the house. Those kennels are just breeding grounds for kennel cough, even if they're vaccinated. You know, you can get this special enzyme spray for dog beds that completely neutralizes the wet dog smell. You just mist it on and let it air dry, but you have to make sure you get the kind with the live enzymes, not just the perfumes that cover it up. The perfumed ones just make the house smell like a wet dog rolled in a lavender bush, which is honestly worse than just the dog smell to begin with. I bought a bottle for my sister's house because her schnauzer...\n\nOh, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Right, my speech. Yes, people do say they can't follow me when I try to explain things. They say I jump around too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that your emotions seem flatter than they used to?\n\nPatient: Flatter? No, I don't think my emotions are flat at all. I still feel things just as strongly as I always have. Like when I get frustrated, I really feel it. Just yesterday, I was so irritated because I went to restock my cleaning caddy, and someone—I'm looking at my husband—had taken my good microfiber cloths out to the garage to wipe down his truck. \n\nYou cannot use the good indoor microfiber on automotive grease! It completely ruins the fibers. Once you get that black grease in them, they're done for. You can wash them in hot water with degreaser all you want, but they'll just streak the mirrors next time you try to clean a bathroom. I buy those specific blue cloths in bulk from a commercial supply place downtown because they have a tighter weave than the ones you get at the grocery store. The grocery store ones leave little lint fuzzies all over the glass, especially if you use them with a vinegar solution instead of regular glass cleaner. I always tell my younger girls at the agency, if you're going to clean a mirror, you have to fold the cloth into quarters so you have eight clean sides to work with. If you just bunch it up in your hand, you're just smearing the toothpaste splatters around...\n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, my emotions. No, they aren't flat. I still feel everything just fine.\n\nDoctor: Besides that woman's voice, have you heard any other voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Other voices? No, it's just her. Even when the house is completely quiet, I hear her right near the kitchen window. She tells me to check the locks or move my husband's pills, and it's as clear as if she were standing right next to me. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen window, I really need to get up there and clean the tracks before winter sets in. The dust just collects in those little grooves, and once the condensation starts building up from the cold weather, it turns into this awful, sticky mud. You really need an old toothbrush to get into the corners properly. My grandmother used to save all her old toothbrushes for cleaning grout and window tracks. She had this whole jar of them under the sink, right next to the Borax. You know, Borax is a miracle worker for getting stains out of porcelain sinks. You just make a little paste with some water and let it sit for ten minutes. It works so much better than bleach, and it doesn't ruin the finish. Bleach will actually yellow white porcelain over time if you use it too much. I see it all the time in these older homes where the owners think they have to bleach everything to death just to feel clean. It's terrible for the plumbing, too, especially if you have a septic system...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the voices. Yes, just that one woman by the window telling me about the locks and the pills. No one else.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, just what I mentioned about the locks and the pills. She doesn't order me to do anything else, if that's what you mean. I'm always checking locks anyway, especially when I'm finishing up at a client's house. \n\nThe Millers just put in one of those fancy smart locks on their front door where you punch in a code instead of using a key. But honestly, they are an absolute nightmare to keep clean. The oils from people's fingers leave these shiny smudges all over the matte black finish. I tried telling Mrs. Miller that a burglar could just look at the greasy spots and guess her code! You can't just wipe it with a wet rag, either, because it streaks terribly. You really need a tiny bit of rubbing alcohol on a utility cloth. And you have to be so careful not to let any liquid seep behind the buttons because it'll short out the whole electrical mechanism. My brother-in-law ruined his garage door keypad exactly like that. He just sprayed the hose right at it while he was power-washing the driveway, and he had to pay two hundred dollars to get a guy out there to replace the whole circuit board... \n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Did you ask about the locks? Oh, the voice. Right. No, she doesn't tell me to do anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, definitely not. My thoughts are completely my own. Though I will say my mind wanders a lot when I'm working on autopilot. \n\nLike when I'm doing baseboards. You spend a lot of time on your hands and knees doing baseboards, and you really have to use a damp rag, not one of those fluffy dusters. A duster just pushes the dog hair right back into the air, and then it just settles onto the coffee table you just finished polishing. I always tell the new girls at work, you need a small bucket of warm water with just one single drop of Dawn dish soap. Dawn cuts right through that sticky, greasy dust that builds up, especially on the baseboards near the kitchen. But you have to wring the rag out really, really well, or you'll warp the MDF if they have cheap baseboards. So many of these new houses use that cheap pressed wood instead of real solid pine, and the minute water gets under the paint, it swells up like a sponge. I saw it happen at a beautiful new build over in the Heights—the whole hallway trim was completely ruined because the homeowners kept mopping with way too much water on their sponge mop...\n\nI'm sorry, my mind is just running today. Did you ask about my thoughts? Right, no, nobody is controlling them. Just me in there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you had more trouble keeping up with your work or daily responsibilities than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The agency I work for, they took away my supervisory routes recently. They said the clients and the other girls felt they couldn't rely on my work the way they used to. Which is just ridiculous, because I have always been the most thorough one on the team. \n\nBut it's funny they brought up reliability, because reliability is everything when you're cleaning someone's home. You have to show up on time, and you have to remember exactly how they like things done. Mrs. Gable, for instance, she insists her hardwood floors are only cleaned with this special almond oil polish she orders from Germany. You can't use Murphy's Oil Soap, you can't use vinegar and water, it has to be this almond oil. And it smells wonderful, like a bakery, but it makes the floors incredibly slick if you don't buff it out perfectly. I always tell the girls, you have to use a microfiber cloth on the bottom of a flat mop, and you have to go with the grain of the wood. If you go against the grain, you leave these awful streaks that catch the light from the windows. And she has those huge floor-to-ceiling windows in her sunroom, so you see every single flaw. I swear, cleaning windows is an art form in itself. You can't wash windows on a sunny day, you know. The sun dries the cleaner too fast and leaves streaks. You always have to wait for an overcast day...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Trouble keeping up? Right. Well, it's not just work. At home, my husband and our pastor have had to step in a lot more lately. They say I'm not keeping up with my household chores and caregiving safely. I don't know about \"safely,\" but I suppose I have been falling behind a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Speaking less? Oh, no, definitely not. If anything, my husband says I can talk the ear off a brass monkey. I just get going, you know, especially when I'm trying to explain something important to the clients. 

Like just last week, I was trying to tell Mr. Henderson about his shower grout. People always think they should use pure bleach on grout to get the mildew out, but bleach actually breaks down the sealant over time. It makes it porous, so the next time it gets wet, the mold just grows deeper into the crevices. You end up with this awful pink bacteria that you can never scrub out, no matter how hard you try with an old toothbrush. I always tell the girls at the agency, you need a hydrogen peroxide and baking soda paste. You just mix it up in a little glass bowl—never metal, because it reacts—and you let it sit for exactly ten minutes before you scrub it with a stiff nylon brush. The nylon is important because wire brushes will scratch the ceramic tiles, and once you scratch the glaze on a tile, it just collects dirt like a magnet. I saw a whole master bathroom in a beautiful mid-century house completely ruined because the owners hired some cheap handyman who took a wire wheel to the shower walls... 

Oh, my goodness, listen to me. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Speaking less? No, I definitely haven't had any trouble talking. I talk just as much as I always have.

Just yesterday, I was so irritated because I went to restock my cleaning caddy, and someone—I'm looking at my husband—had taken my good microfiber cloths out to the garage to wipe down his truck. \n\nYou cannot use the good indoor microfiber on automotive grease! It completely ruins the fibers. Once you get that black grease in them, they're done for. You can wash them in hot water with degreaser all you want, but they'll just streak the mirrors next time you try to clean a bathroom. I buy those specific blue cloths in bulk from a commercial supply place downtown because they have a tighter weave than the ones you get at the grocery store. The grocery store ones leave little lint fuzzies all over the glass, especially if you use them with a vinegar solution instead of regular glass cleaner. I always tell my younger girls at the agency, if you're going to clean a mirror, you have to fold the cloth into quarters so you have eight clean sides to work with. If you just bunch it up in your hand, you're just smearing the toothpaste splatters around...\n\nI'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. What did you ask me? Oh, right, my emotions. No, they aren't flat. I still feel everything just fine.\n\nDoctor: Besides that woman's voice, have you heard any other voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Other voices? No, it's just her. Even when the house is completely quiet, I hear her right near the kitchen window. She tells me to check the locks or move my husband's pills, and it's as clear as if she were standing right next to me. \n\nSpeaking of the kitchen window, I really need to get up there and clean the tracks before winter sets in. The dust just collects in those little grooves, and once the condensation starts building up from the cold weather, it turns into this awful, sticky mud. You really need an old toothbrush to get into the corners properly. My grandmother used to save all her old toothbrushes for cleaning grout and window tracks. She had this whole jar of them under the sink, right next to the Borax. You know, Borax is a miracle worker for getting stains out of porcelain sinks. You just make a little paste with some water and let it sit for ten minutes. It works so much better than bleach, and it doesn't ruin the finish. Bleach will actually yellow white porcelain over time if you use it too much. I see it all the time in these older homes where the owners think they have to bleach everything to death just to feel clean. It's terrible for the plumbing, too, especially if you have a septic system...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, the voices. Yes, just that one woman by the window telling me about the locks and the pills. No one else.\n\nDoctor: Does the woman's voice ever tell you to do something?\n\nPatient: Well, just what I mentioned about the locks and the pills. She doesn't order me to do anything else, if that's what you mean. I'm always checking locks anyway, especially when I'm finishing up at a client's house. \n\nThe Millers just put in one of those fancy smart locks on their front door where you punch in a code instead of using a key. But honestly, they are an absolute nightmare to keep clean. The oils from people's fingers leave these shiny smudges all over the matte black finish. I tried telling Mrs. Miller that a burglar could just look at the greasy spots and guess her code! You can't just wipe it with a wet rag, either, because it streaks terribly. You really need a tiny bit of rubbing alcohol on a utility cloth. And you have to be so careful not to let any liquid seep behind the buttons because it'll short out the whole electrical mechanism. My brother-in-law ruined his garage door keypad exactly like that. He just sprayed the hose right at it while he was power-washing the driveway, and he had to pay two hundred dollars to get a guy out there to replace the whole circuit board... \n\nI'm sorry, what did you ask me? Did you ask about the locks? Oh, the voice. Right. No, she doesn't tell me to do anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that someone was controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? No, definitely not. My thoughts are completely my own. Though I will say my mind wanders a lot when I'm working on autopilot. \n\nLike when I'm doing baseboards. You spend a lot of time on your hands and knees doing baseboards, and you really have to use a damp rag, not one of those fluffy dusters. A duster just pushes the dog hair right back into the air, and then it just settles onto the coffee table you just finished polishing. I always tell the new girls at work, you need a small bucket of warm water with just one single drop of Dawn dish soap. Dawn cuts right through that sticky, greasy dust that builds up, especially on the baseboards near the kitchen. But you have to wring the rag out really, really well, or you'll warp the MDF if they have cheap baseboards. So many of these new houses use that cheap pressed wood instead of real solid pine, and the minute water gets under the paint, it swells up like a sponge. I saw it happen at a beautiful new build over in the Heights—the whole hallway trim was completely ruined because the homeowners kept mopping with way too much water on their sponge mop...\n\nI'm sorry, my mind is just running today. Did you ask about my thoughts? Right, no, nobody is controlling them. Just me in there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, have you had more trouble keeping up with your work or daily responsibilities than you used to?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. The agency I work for, they took away my supervisory routes recently. They said the clients and the other girls felt they couldn't rely on my work the way they used to. Which is just ridiculous, because I have always been the most thorough one on the team. \n\nBut it's funny they brought up reliability, because reliability is everything when you're cleaning someone's home. You have to show up on time, and you have to remember exactly how they like things done. Mrs. Gable, for instance, she insists her hardwood floors are only cleaned with this special almond oil polish she orders from Germany. You can't use Murphy's Oil Soap, you can't use vinegar and water, it has to be this almond oil. And it smells wonderful, like a bakery, but it makes the floors incredibly slick if you don't buff it out perfectly. I always tell the girls, you have to use a microfiber cloth on the bottom of a flat mop, and you have to go with the grain of the wood. If you go against the grain, you leave these awful streaks that catch the light from the windows. And she has those huge floor-to-ceiling windows in her sunroom, so you see every single flaw. I swear, cleaning windows is an art form in itself. You can't wash windows on a sunny day, you know. The sun dries the cleaner too fast and leaves streaks. You always have to wait for an overcast day...\n\nOh, dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What was the question? Trouble keeping up? Right. Well, it's not just work. At home, my husband and our pastor have had to step in a lot more lately. They say I'm not keeping up with my household chores and caregiving safely. I don't know about \"safely,\" but I suppose I have been falling behind a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself speaking much less than you used to?\n\nPatient: Speaking less? Oh, no, definitely not. If anything, my husband says I can talk the ear off a brass monkey. I just get going, you know, especially when I'm trying to explain something important to the clients. \n\nLike just last week, I was trying to tell Mr. Henderson about his shower grout. People always think they should use pure bleach on grout to get the mildew out, but bleach actually breaks down the sealant over time. It makes it porous, so the next time it gets wet, the mold just grows deeper into the crevices. You end up with this awful pink bacteria that you can never scrub out, no matter how hard you try with an old toothbrush. I always tell the girls at the agency, you need a hydrogen peroxide and baking soda paste. You just mix it up in a little glass bowl—never metal, because it reacts—and you let it sit for exactly ten minutes before you scrub it with a stiff nylon brush. The nylon is important because wire brushes will scratch the ceramic tiles, and once you scratch the glaze on a tile, it just collects dirt like a magnet. I saw a whole master bathroom in a beautiful mid-century house completely ruined because the owners hired some cheap handyman who took a wire wheel to the shower walls... \n\nOh, my goodness, listen to me. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Speaking less? No, I definitely haven't had any trouble talking. I talk just as much as I always have.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I don't think so. The voice and the feeling of being watched, they always seem to happen together. It's like they're a package deal. 

Which reminds me of those cleaning supply bundles they sell at the big box stores. You end up with this awful pink bacteria that you can never scrub out, no matter how hard you try with an old toothbrush. You just mix it up in a little glass bowl—never metal, because it reacts—and you let it sit for exactly ten minutes before you scrub it with a stiff nylon brush. The nylon is important because wire brushes will scratch the ceramic tiles, and once you scratch the glaze on a tile, it just collects dirt like a magnet. I saw a whole master bathroom in a beautiful mid-century house completely ruined because the owners hired some cheap handyman who took a wire wheel to the shower walls... \n\nOh, my goodness, listen to me. I'm doing it again. What did you ask me? Speaking less? No, I definitely haven't had any trouble talking. I talk just as much as I always have.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least one month when you only felt people were watching you, without hearing the woman's voice?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so.
